The true-minus-flat derivative contribution is absorbed directly by wH bulk with the extra d2g . The
exponent is explicitly −1 + .3 + max(.55, .471) = −.15. Thus these shell costs are summable with
radius gain, explaining why no extra positive-q weighted bad flux is required at the weak level. All
raw wave sources in this multiplier now use (A.17); the signed leading term in (A.16) just uses the
flat good/bad weight gap here.
Claim 3: the flat commutators are triangular. To specify the flat terms, commute Z after
lab differentiations, eliminating Pg at lower orders; separate the P [H] commutations, including

                                                  126
any scalar commutator multiples of it, into the nonflat sources below. The flat recurrence is
Pη S = (S + 1)Pη + r−2 As ; rotation commutator errors are r−2 O(s)(1, Z, Z 2 ). Thus each S count
contributes +(1 + s · n)Lχ/r from the pure S 2 leading term on the right, giving λ1 in (A.16). Terms
where extra rotations differentiate its s-dependent coefficient can instead be counted as O(s). All
remaining size-one terms in fflat cost, after multiplication by r, good derivatives/field-over-radius of
lower orders or of the same order with fewer S’s, with unchanged j; feedback terms at this precision
have O(s) coefficients. In particular a pure r−2 χ term at the same order from a commuted S uses
r−1 χ = Lχprior . This proves their triangular acceptability.
Claim 4: the boundary costs use the stated inputs. In this argument entries involving
the outer cap supremum need only be estimated beyond R0 . In the averaging annulus of the
initial radial cutoff all field bulk/time-slice controls can instead use the compact estimates with
constants depending on R0 , which have full mixed orders there. For instance every common principal
commutator puts order at most Ni +1 on the differentiated field (one lower commutation count before
the two wave derivatives); in the D-equation an additional order-two term on h uses at most nine
derivatives but carries either a parameter acceleration coefficient or a strong field derivative. Thus
field source/current products there can use ordinary time-weighted Sobolev estimates off trapping,
not an outgoing cap input on the overlap. On linear pure-defect forcing involving acceleration alone
one keeps the direct far a  ̃ bound with its same radius gain on Sa , not a large overlap coefficient
times Sa . The required direct source estimate is proved in Section B. The surface cutoffs themselves
can be selected by averaging against the full time annular bounds, irrespective of the outer cap
used. Divergence integrations at infinity can also first take a sequence of radial truncations at
each bounded time range; ordinary high symbol decay there suffices on the raw jets (with pH < 1,
and an extra decay power for the strong field), and the displayed cap/bulk bounds on the specific
corrections, or their bounded-time symbol estimates, suffice for correction fluxes.
Claim 5: finite hierarchical absorption. Add the two multiplier estimates with fixed coercive
relative factors, use Hardy and the true-to-flat conversions, and take separate suprema over the
endpoints. Enumerate the finitely many blocks in the stated prior order as 1, . . . , J and let Ek
denote their full norms. After Young’s inequality each block has an estimate of the form
                                                J
                                                ∑               ∑
                                 Ek2 ≤ ϵ(R0 )         El2 + C         El2 + B 2 ,
                                                l=1             l<k

where B is the nonhierarchical right-hand budget in (A.19); the same argument applies to tunably
small uniform errors. All size-one constants C and the number J are independent of the two large
matching radii. Choose fixed positive weights ak decreasing so rapidly that C k>l ak ≤ al /4 for
                                                                               ∑

every l. Summing ak Ek2 absorbs every prior-order term. Only after these weights have been chosen
do we enlarge R0 so that ϵ(R0 ) k ak ≤ 14 mink ak . The resulting weighted norm is equivalent to
                                 ∑

the finite sum defining F, with constants independent of the matching radii, and its square root
gives (A.20). Small multiples of any same-time right hand budgets in the bootstrap can be kept
on the right before combining all levels. Compact boundary/source squares costing C(R0 )K2R are
harmless with these conventions. The source hypotheses are verified in Section B, yielding (B.7).
This proves the stated energy reduction.


B     Far-source estimates and uniform coupling
We verify all source and current-correction hypotheses of Proposition A.6 for the lab equation of
Lemma 6.1 and its differentiated D equation, on an arbitrary smooth finite slab. In particular

                                                      127
the background coefficients have the stated symbol and D gains, the exact lab gauge gives the
differentiated weak-null polarization, and Lemma 6.2 supplies the initial and overlap bounds. The
quantities from Section A, including the news estimate (A.12), are consequences of the same small
bootstrap used here. They are not assumptions about an already global evolution. We write N for
a fixed multiple of the total bootstrap size, allowing also the input size in this coefficient. The three
target levels are (Ui , Ni ) = (h, 9), (h, 8), (ψ, 7) for i = 0, H, P respectively. The coefficients, source
splittings and current corrections below are always expressed in Cartesian laboratory components
before conversion to the emitter frame.
    The source budgets are (A.17) and the mixed charge (A.18), with the bulk measure ∥ · ∥b =
  2
L (dt dr dω/r) from Section A.7. For each equation, P is the sum of its corrections with their actual
signs. The mixed charge uses only the corrected ξX cap in (A.15); it requires no M -weighted bound
on an arbitrary uncorrected good derivative.

B.2    The complete source catalogue
Proposition B.1 (Complete far-source estimate). For each commuted equation on such a finite
slab, the sum of its budgets (A.17), together with its mixed charges (A.18), is bounded, for some
fixed c1 > 0, by
                                           ∑
                 oR0 →∞ (1)(F + Sa ) + C           Fprior + C(R0 )KR + Cfix (ein + N1+c1 ).            (B.6)
                                           prior

The finite prior order is: level P before the two h hierarchies; within each level lower total order
first, then more laboratory times at equal total order, then fewer dilations S. Equal-index norms are
aggregated. The constants on prior far quantities are independent of both matching radii. Small
radial gains and nonlinear bootstrap products may cross this order. After fixed finite hierarchical
weighting, Proposition A.6 therefore gives, for some c2 > 0,

                      F ≤ oR0 →∞ (1)(F + Sa ) + C(R0 )KR + Cfix (ein + N1+c2 ).                        (B.7)

The initial and starting-annulus correction costs satisfy the same bound. In particular the pure
acceleration defect on the starting annulus has its direct small radial coefficient on Sa .

retain the coefficient placements, corrections, and endpoint estimates needed for each row.

Proof. We verify the catalogue in six claims. Coefficients denoted by N are bootstrap/input factors,
so N F is nonlinear. The pure background symbol bound B − η = O(r−1 ) has no such smallness
factor. All wave equations and laboratory derivatives are taken before the emitter decomposition.
Claim 1: common principal commutators. We first derive the common source forms, then
estimate their raw and outgoing budgets separately. The distinction is necessary because the
outgoing correction avoids one bad derivative, whereas the raw budget uses the derivative hierarchy
directly.

one B h replaced by B ψ if the total count on the two inputs is at most seven (measure a high B ψ in
its own level; point B ψ through its stated orders in (B.2)). Thus T j D on the vanishing-coefficient
terms works simply counting D as up to one extra lab first derivative on the metric/field factors;
differentiated ss coefficients are bounded. Additional unscaled spatial lab derivatives can always
be distributed before switching to Z, T counts, using ∂d = ℓd T + r−1 bd Z on lab jets with T still
commuting through other lab derivatives. Smooth bounded field compositions with explicit r−1
suppression and the analogous symbol bounds work by the same allocations.

does not meet this band. If the upper endpoint meets a tile, it is the uniformly smooth graph

meeting r ≥ R0 , the required enlargements and reflected points never extend inward beyond the
fixed-factor overlap below R0 ; near-cutoff boxes may equivalently use compact overlap controls.
Source integrals with an additional cap truncation can be bounded on the full time slab. Summing
with bounded overlap proves the near-region raw budgets, with only the stated compact-overlap
and bootstrap-product costs.
Claim 6: initial and starting-annulus costs. All corrections are added before forming ξ for each
equation; the bounds concern (A.1) for uncorrected fields, already part of the total norm. On fixed
starting overlap annuli their extra current and boundary costs satisfy the same compact convention:
(B.8) corrections use a first derivative of a lower commuted field with bounded coefficients (weighted
at the same time level, with acceleration decay where strong and weak inputs couple); in (B.11)
smooth-acceleration corrections the coefficient carries a  ̃. The products in (B.20) on such annuli
cost quadratically by weak fields with 2AH > A. One can use the off-trap compact controls and
sphere Sobolev placements there. Time-zero costs use the same cap estimates or directly the input
symbol bounds. All patch/annulus constants on nonlinear terms are allowed fixed before smallness.
This verifies (B.6) and therefore (B.7), with every positive-radius-gain term summable and the prior
linear costs taken with independent far constants as specified there.


B.3    Simultaneous exterior closure
The compact, far, and acceleration estimates concern the same finite smooth slab. Write K = KR ,
F = F, and a∗ = Sa , and collect the fixed data and superlinear remainders in E. The three estimates
Proposition 5.5, (B.7), and Equation (5.16) have the form

                                    K ≤ εR (K + F ) + E,
                                    F ≤ ε0 (F + a∗ ) + B0 K + E,                               (B.22)
                                    a∗ ≤ D0 K + C0 F + E.

Here ε0 becomes small by increasing R0 , whereas εR is made small later by the compact matching
radius and plateau choices. To choose them in this order, we need C0 to stay bounded as R0 grows.
The next lemma proves exactly that fact and the compatible treatment of the pure acceleration
defect.

Lemma B.2 (Radius-uniform coupling). The coefficient of F in Equation (5.16) may be taken
independent of R0 . The comparison of the background defect fB with Q(0)a may be split at a radius
Y fixed before R0 : its compact part costs C(Y )KR and fixed data/nonlinear terms, and its tail costs
CY −1+ϵ Sa , with any sufficiently small fixed ϵ > 0. Conversely, the pure far defect, including the
starting annulus, has an oR0 →∞ (1) coefficient on Sa in (B.7).

Corollary B.3 (Global exterior estimates from the base neighborhood). There is a neighborhood
specified solely by sufficiently small p10 difference from the Kerr bridge such that the two reduced
computing-cylinder evolutions are global and smooth. They obey Equations (5.7), (5.16) and (5.17)
and Proposition 5.5 and (B.7), with arbitrarily small total base bounds after the neighborhood is
sufficiently shrunk. The higher smooth norms on growing cylinders have the subexponential bounds of
Proposition 5.6; their constants may depend on the fixed smooth datum and the order. No smallness
of those higher initial norms is required.

Proof. Proposition 6.4 proves the simultaneous absorption and continuation from these estimates.
Its bootstrap cap can be chosen arbitrarily small before the p10 neighborhood is shrunk. This
gives the asserted small base bounds on both cylinders. The higher-order conclusion is then
Proposition 5.6, whose induction uses finite higher initial seminorms without shrinking the common
base neighborhood.


C     A triangular comparison with one common rate
Lemma C.1 (A finite triangular system with one common rate). Fix an integer k ≥ 0 and C0 > 0.
For each T ≥ 1, let 0 < LT ≤ C0 (1 + T ) and let E0 , . . . , Ek be nonnegative absolutely continuous
functions on [0, LT ]. Suppose nonnegative integrable functions rT , bT , S0 , . . . , Sk satisfy, almost
everywhere,
                           Ej′ ≤ rT Ej + bT
                                            ∑
                                              Eℓ + Sj ,          0 ≤ j ≤ k.
                                                 ℓ<j
               ∫t               ∫t                      ∑k                 q
Put RT (t) =   0 rT , BT (t) = 0 bT , and Qk (B) =           q=0 ((k + 1)B) /q!. Then
                                                 (                  ∫ t                        )
                max Ej (t) ≤ eRT (t) Qk (BT (t)) max Ej (0) +             e−RT (s) max Sj (s) ds .
                j≤k                                  j≤k             0            j≤k


In particular, if BT (LT ) ≤ eo(T ) , the polynomial factor is eo(T ) for this fixed k. A bound bT (t) ≤ eo(T )
uniform for 0 ≤ t ≤ LT implies this integrated bound because LT = O(T ).

Proof. Set Ẽj = e−RT Ej . Their differential inequalities have no diagonal term. Let A be the strictly
lower-triangular matrix with entries Ajℓ = 1 for ℓ < j. Comparison with this positive linear system
gives propagator exp((BT (t) − BT (s))A). Since Ak+1 = 0 and ∥A∥ℓ∞ →ℓ∞ ≤ k + 1, its norm is at
most Qk (BT (t)). Variation of constants proves the displayed estimate. Only finitely many nested
lower-order integrations occur; there is one factor eRT (t) .


D     Vacuum constraint correction outside a protected ball
Localized deformation of the vacuum constraints was developed by Corvino, Corvino–Schoen, and
Chruściel–Delay [9–11]; conical localization was established by Carlotto–Schoen [6]. We require a
correction that fixes an inner ball and allows a decaying tail. The explicit inverse below provides
this support property with full derivative gain. A nonlinear solve at one small base norm then gives


                                                       140
smooth exact constraints, even when higher data norms are large. Section 9.6 applies the result to
the rescaled beam seed.
   We use the vacuum constraint map
                            Φ(g, k) = Rg + (trg k)2 − |k|2g , ∇j kij − ∇i trg k .
                                      (                                                    )


The exterior correction problem.                  For a nonnegative integer m and a decay exponent j, put
                          ∥v∥Hjm = ∥v∥H m (B2 ) + sup Lj ∥v(L ·)∥H m ({1<|y|<2}) .                           (D.1)
                                                        L≥1

Componentwise versions of these norms are used for tensors. For an integer s ≥ 3, let
              Y s = H4s (R3 ; R ⊕ T ∗ R3 ),         X s = H1s+2 (R3 ; Sym2 ) ⊕ H2s+1 (R3 ; Sym2 ).
The Euclidean linearization of Φ, denoted only in this subsection by P , is
                                              (                                     )
                                P (q, p) = ∂i ∂j qij − ∆qii , ∂j pij − ∂i pjj .                              (D.2)
Proposition D.1 (Exact correction outside a protected ball). Fix an integer s ≥ 3. There are
constants ϵs , Cs > 0 with the following property. Let dB = (δ + aB , bB ) be smooth metric and second
fundamental form data on R3 , with δ + aB positive definite and, for every multi-index α,
                       |∂ α aB (y)| ≤ Cα ⟨y⟩−1−|α| ,              |∂ α bB (y)| ≤ Cα ⟨y⟩−2−|α| .
Let ϕ be a smooth compactly supported scalar/covector pair such that
                            supp ϕ ⊂ {|y| ≥ 1},            Φ(dB ) = ϕ         on |y| ≥ 1.
Define the two base sizes
                              βs = ∥aB ∥H s+2 + ∥bB ∥H s+1 ,               ηs = ∥ϕ∥Y s .
                                              1               2

If βs + ηs < ϵs , there is a smooth tensor pair vc = (qc , pc ) such that
             supp vc ⊂ {|y| ≥ 1},         Φ(dB + vc ) = 0            on |y| ≥ 1,        ∥vc ∥X s ≤ Cs ηs .
The corrected metric remains positive definite. The correction is smooth across |y| = 1 and satisfies
symbol bounds of metric weight one and second fundamental form weight two at every order. No
smallness is required of these higher bounds. No moment condition is imposed on ϕ, and the correction
need not be compactly supported; its tail may change the asymptotic charges. No constraint equation
is asserted for the artificially supplied datum on |y| < 1.
   The proof first constructs an exterior right inverse with full derivative gain. Its definition on all
inputs, not only supported ones, will permit smoothness to be proved by rotations and dilations at
the same base order.
Lemma D.2 (Exterior right inverse for the flat constraints). There is a linear operator K, defined
on every Y s and bounded Y s → X s for each integer s ≥ 3, such that
                   supp z ⊂ {|y| ≥ 1}         =⇒      P Kz = z,            supp Kz ⊂ {|y| ≥ 1}.              (D.3)
The same construction is used at all orders. Its conjugates by rotations and positive dilations of the
argument are smooth in the operator norm from Y s to X s .
Proof. We construct the inverse in three steps. Newton potentials solve the flat constraints and
supply the permitted tail. Cutting them off on the inner ball creates a compact error whose
Euclidean Killing moments vanish automatically. An annulus-supported primitive removes that
error without changing the tail. Only this compact error has moment conditions; the original source
is unrestricted.

                                                        141
The Newton tail and its cutoff error. First construct an inverse without a support restriction.
For z = (f, F ), let
                                −2∆u = f,      ∆Wi = Fi
be the Newton potentials, and set

                            q0,ij = uδij ,       p0,ij = ∂i Wj + ∂j Wi − 21 δij ∂k Wk .                    (D.4)

Then the scalar component of P (q0 , p0 ) is −2∆u. Also p0,jj = 12 ∂j Wj , so the gradient terms in the
momentum component cancel and that component is ∆Wi . Hence P (q0 , p0 ) = z.
    Scaled Sobolev embedding gives |z(y)| ≤ Cs ⟨y⟩−4 ∥z∥Y s . Splitting the Newton integral into a
neighborhood of the origin, a neighborhood of y, and their complement yields

                                      |u(y)| + |W (y)| ≤ Cs ⟨y⟩−1 ∥z∥Y s .

For completeness, on |v| < |y|/2 the Newton kernel is O(|y|−1 ) and the source has bounded total
integral; on |v − y| < |y|/2, its integral against the source is O(|y|−2 ); the remaining region has
the same or a smaller bound. Local estimates on a fixed ball handle bounded y. On an annulus of
radius L, the rescaled right-hand side of the potential equation has H s norm O(L−2 ∥z∥Y s ). Interior
elliptic estimates on a finite cover by slightly larger rescaled balls therefore give

                              ∥u(L ·)∥H s+2 + ∥W (L ·)∥H s+2 ≤ Cs L−1 ∥z∥Y s .

One physical derivative of W introduces an additional factor L−1 . Consequently

                                             ∥(q0 , p0 )∥X s ≤ Cs ∥z∥Y s .                                 (D.5)

   Choose a smooth radial χ which is zero on |y| ≤ 1.2 and one on |y| ≥ 1.8. With
                                                       (                   )
                                             G = P (χ − 1)(q0 , p0 )

we have P (χ(q0 , p0 )) = z +G. The pair (χ−1)(q0 , p0 ) defining G is compactly supported. Integration
by parts against affine functions and Euclidean Killing fields gives
             ∫                ∫                    ∫                   ∫
                 Gsc = 0,         ya Gsc = 0,          Gvec,a = 0,         (ya Gvec,b − yb Gvec,a ) = 0.   (D.6)

Indeed two derivatives annihilate an affine scalar; one derivative annihilates a translation, while
pairing the divergence of a symmetric tensor with a rotation gives zero. If z is supported in {|y| ≥ 1},
then G = −z = 0 in |y| < 1, and G = 0 in |y| > 1.8. It is therefore supported in

                                                A = {1 ≤ |y| ≤ 2}.

We next give a compactly supported primitive for every pair on A satisfying (D.6).
     A divergence primitive on the annulus. We use a capwise version of Bogovskii’s divergence
primitive [4], whose support and regularity properties are treated by Costabel–McIntosh [12]. The
finite mass transfers below adapt the construction to the annulus. Fix a unit-mass bump ζ0 supported
strictly inside A. There is a linear operator D, bounded from H m to H m+1 on inputs localized to a
fixed compact set for each integer m ≥ 0, such that, for every scalar e supported in A,
                                                           ∫
                                  ∂i (De)i = e − ζ0             e,   supp De ⊂ A.                          (D.7)


                                                               142
Here is the construction and the derivative estimate.
   Cover A by sufficiently narrow angular caps and take smooth compactly supported cutoffs ψa
whose sum is one on A. They can be chosen so that their sum is a fixed cutoff η equal to one on
A, with support in 1/2 < |y| < 3. For each cap choose a unit-mass bump ζa near radius 3/2 in its
central direction. Make the cap and bump small enough that

                                               b · v̂ > 1,                     |b| < 2

whenever v ∈ A ∩ supp ψa and b ∈ supp ζa . Every point on the segment from v to b then has
projection at least one on v̂, and lies in the radius-two ball by convexity. The whole segment is thus
contained in A.
   For a smooth compactly supported scalar e, define
                                        ∫                         ∫ ∞
                                                                            ζ v + t(x − v) t2 dt dv.
                                                                               (                        )
                          (Dζ e)(x) =       e(v)(x − v)                                                                               (D.8)
                                                                      1

Its support is contained in the union of the segments joining supp e to supp ζ: if the integrand is
nonzero, then x = (1 − t−1 )v + t−1 b for some b ∈ supp ζ. Testing against a gradient and changing
variables b = v + t(x − v) gives
                             ∫                 ∫              ∫
                                                                               (                    )
                                 Dζ e · ∇φ =           e(v)       ζ(b) φ(b) − φ(v) db dv.

In this calculation, the substitution τ = t−1 reduces the inner integral
                                                                    ∫
                                                                         to the integral of the derivative
of φ(v + τ (b − v)) for 0 ≤ τ ≤ 1. It follows that div Dζ e = e − ζ e.
    The bumps may be chosen as translates of one small bump. Join their centers to that of ζ0 along
paths at radius 3/2, and choose the common bump small enough that every translated support
remains inside the open annulus. If γa is such a path from the center of ζa to the center of ζ0 , then
                                               ∫ 1
                                    Va (x) =            γa′ (τ )ζbase (x − γa (τ )) dτ
                                                   0

is smooth, compactly supported in the open annulus, and satisfies div Va = ζa − ζ0 . Define
                                             ∑                                 ∑            ∫
                                     De =          Dζa (ψa e) +                        Va       ψa e.
                                               a                                   a

For annulus-supported e this proves (D.7). The same formula defines D on arbitrary inputs after
the fixed localization by the ψa . Its values are always supported in a fixed compact set, although
exactness and annulus support are only needed and asserted on the indicated inputs.
   To prove the one-derivative bound, write r = |x − v| and ω = (x − v)/r. The kernel in (D.8) is
              ∫ ∞                                                                                           ∫ ∞
       ωr−2         ζ(v + τ ω)τ 2 dτ = ωr−2 a(v, ω) + E(x, v),                                  a(v, ω) =         ζ(v + τ ω)τ 2 dτ.
              r                                                                                             0

On fixed compact sets a is smooth, and
                                                              ∫ 1
                                                                          ζ v + t(x − v) t2 dt.
                                                                           (                       )
                                 E(x, v) = −(x − v)
                                                                  0

This remainder is jointly smooth on fixed compact sets, with bounds controlled by smooth seminorms
of the bump. The leading kernel has degree −2 in x − v. Its distributional x derivative is a bounded
multiplication term plus a principal-value kernel of degree −3, whose angular mean is zero. To see

                                                              143
the cancellation, integrate the ordinary derivative over a concentric annulus: the two boundary
fluxes agree by degree −2 homogeneity, so the coefficient of the radial logarithm is zero. Expansion
of its smooth angular dependence in spherical harmonics writes it as a summable series of mean-zero
angular convolution kernels acting on the input multiplied by smooth coefficient functions of v.
These coefficient functions have rapidly decreasing bounds in the harmonic degree. The classical L2
estimate for a truncated homogeneous mean-zero singular integral, with norm controlled by finitely
many angular seminorms, therefore bounds the differentiated kernel on L2 . The undifferentiated
weak singularity is integrable, and the portions away from the diagonal are smooth on bounded sets.
This proves
                                          ∥De∥H 1 ≤ C∥e∥L2
for the fixed localization.
    Higher orders follow without loss by simultaneous translations. For the unlocalized formula,

                                        ∂j Dζ e = Dζ (∂j e) + D∂j ζ e.

The same kernel estimate applies to derivatives of the bump, without a unit-mass requirement.
Iterating this identity and differentiating the fixed cutoffs and finite-rank terms gives

                                  ∥De∥H m+1 ≤ Cm ∥e∥H m                (m ≥ 0)                                (D.9)

for the localized operator. Approximation extends the formula and its support assertions from
smooth inputs to supported Sobolev inputs.
   Symmetric divergence and the scalar primitive. Let F ′ be supported in A, with zero translation
and rotation moments. Set
                                         Tij = (DFi′ )j .
Then ∂j Tij = Fi′ , and integration by parts gives
                                 ∫                    ∫
                                     (Tij − Tji ) =       (yi Fj′ − yj Fi′ ) = 0.

Define

                                     Hijk = 21 (Dijk − Djki + Dkij ),
             (            )
     Dijk = D(Tij − Tji ) k ,                                                        Sij = Tij − ∂k Hijk .   (D.10)

Since Dijk = −Djik , direct expansion gives

                                Hijk = −Hikj ,            Hijk − Hjik = Dijk .

Thus S is symmetric, and
                                      ∂j Sij = Fi′ − ∂j ∂k Hijk = Fi′ .
All tensors remain supported in A. The two applications of D and the one derivative in (D.10) show
that this symmetric divergence primitive maps H m to H m+1 . The inverse trace reversal

                                            pij = Sij − 12 δij Skk

satisfies pij − δij pkk = Sij , and is the desired momentum primitive.
    Now let f ′ be supported in A, with zero constant and linear moments. Put Y = Df ′ . Then
                                                   ∫               ∫
                                           ′
                                 ∂i Yi = f ,              Ya = −       ya f ′ = 0.


                                                       144
Set                       ∫
                Mab =         (ya Yb − yb Ya ),           Cab = − 21 Mab ,             Ŷi = Yi + ∂j (Cij ζ0 ).

The skew-symmetry of C implies
                                                                        ∫
                                                 ∂i Ŷi = f ′ ,             Ŷi = 0.

Moreover,                            ∫
                                         (                                       )
                                             ya ∂j (Cbj ζ0 ) − yb ∂j (Caj ζ0 ) = 2Cab ,

so every rotation moment of Ŷ vanishes. Apply the symmetric divergence primitive to Ŷ , obtaining
S with ∂j Sij = Ŷi , and put qij = Sij − 12 δij Skk . Then

                                              ∂i ∂j qij − ∆qii = ∂i ∂j Sij = f ′ .

This gains two derivatives and preserves support in A.
   Together these constructions give an operator B satisfying

               P B(f ′ , F ′ ) = (f ′ , F ′ ),        ∥B(f ′ , F ′ )∥H s+2 ⊕H s+1 ≤ Cs ∥(f ′ , F ′ )∥H s ⊕H s     (D.11)

on the supported moment-free pairs, with output supported in A. They also define a bounded
operator on arbitrary compact inputs: use the fixed localization in every occurrence of D, and
symmetrize the final tensors. On moment-free annulus-supported inputs this final symmetrization
changes nothing. Off that subspace no exactness is asserted or required.
   We may now set
                                     Kz = χ(q0 , p0 ) − BG.                              (D.12)
The estimates already proved give its boundedness on all Y s . For exterior-supported inputs, both
the support assertion and P Kz = z follow from (D.11) and P (χ(q0 , p0 )) = z + G.
   Finally, all the operators have smooth conjugates under the stated group actions. For the
argument dilation (Tλ e)(y) = e(λy), substitution in (D.8) gives

                                    Tλ Dζ Tλ−1 = λDζλ ,                 ζλ (y) = λ3 ζ(λy).                        (D.13)

The transformed cutoffs, path fields, and moment functionals likewise depend smoothly on λ.
Rotations transform the bumps and cutoffs smoothly and multiply tensor components by rotation
matrices. The kernel bounds above are controlled by finitely many smooth seminorms on fixed
compact sets, so Taylor’s formula for these parameter-dependent coefficients proves smoothness in
the operator norm with the full derivative gain. Newton inversion conjugates by the factor λ2 , and
its first derivative by the corresponding factor λ; it is equivariant under rotations. Composition in
(D.12) proves the final assertion.

Proof of Proposition D.1. The same fixed point will supply both the base estimate and the all-order
regularity.

A contraction at one base order.                    Use the operator K from Lemma D.2. Define

                                 N (q, p) = Φ(dB + (q, p)) − Φ(dB ) − P (q, p).                                   (D.14)

For small βs and on a sufficiently small X s ball this is a smooth map to Y s . We record the weights
and derivatives to justify this assertion. Metric perturbations have weight one, their first and

                                                                  145
second derivatives have weights two and three, and second fundamental form perturbations and
their first derivatives have weights two and three. After subtraction of P , the scalar terms are linear
combinations, with smooth bounded inverse-metric factors, of terms with the budgets

                    a ∂ 2 a : 1 + 3 = 4,     (∂a)2 : 2 + 2 = 4,       b2 : 2 + 2 = 4.

The corresponding momentum budgets are

                               a ∂b : 1 + 3 = 4,         (∂a)b : 2 + 2 = 4.

Here a, b may be background or correction factors, with the background-only terms canceled in
(D.14). In particular, a coefficient in front of a highest derivative differs from its Euclidean value by
a weight-one factor. Applying the Sobolev product estimates on the unit ball and on the rescaled
annuli, using s ≥ 3, yields

                ∥N (v) − N (w)∥Y s ≤ Cs (βs + R)∥v − w∥X s ,         ∥v∥X s , ∥w∥X s ≤ R.         (D.15)

The same products after differentiation with respect to v prove smoothness. Inverse-metric factors
are smooth because the metrics in this ball remain uniformly positive. Notice that the assertion
concerns N ; the uncanceled Euclidean leading terms of P have weight three on a general element of
X s.
    Let CK,s be the norm of K : Y s → X s . For sufficiently small βs , ηs , the equation

                                           zc = −ϕ − N (Kzc )                                     (D.16)

is a contraction on ∥zc ∥Y s ≤ 2ηs . Indeed take R = 2CK,s ηs in (D.15) and require Cs CK,s (βs + R) <
1/2. Starting the iteration at zero preserves support in {|y| ≥ 1}: ϕ has that support, Lemma D.2
gives it for every correction, and N (v) = 0 pointwise wherever v vanishes on an open set. The limit
therefore has the same support. With vc = Kzc ,

                         ∥vc ∥X s ≤ 2CK,s ηs ,     Φ(dB + vc ) = 0    on |y| ≥ 1.                 (D.17)

The equation holds there because P vc = zc and Φ(dB ) = ϕ there. On the inner side vc = 0.

Higher smoothness from rotations and dilations. It remains to establish smoothness at
every order, without imposing smallness of all high Sobolev norms. We do this at the single base
order s. Let Ug and Vg be the actions of a rotation and a positive argument dilation on tensor
pairs and on scalar/covector pairs respectively. Rotations act on components as well as arguments;
dilations here act on the argument alone. These actions are uniformly bounded on X s , Y s for g
near the identity. Set

                      Kg = Ug KVg−1 ,       ϕg = Vg ϕ,       Ng (w) = Vg N (Ug−1 w).

The operator family Kg is norm-smooth by Lemma D.2. All background coefficients have the
assumed smooth symbol bounds, and the source is smooth and compactly supported. Thus g |→ ϕg
is smooth in Y s . Writing Ng using the weighted products displayed above shows that it is jointly
smooth in g, w: parameter derivatives fall on the smooth background coefficients or introduce the
explicit scale and component factors in a transformed derivative. They do not differentiate the
unknown w beyond the derivatives already allowed in X s .
    Consider the equation on the full space X s ,

                                        w + Kg ϕg + Kg Ng (w) = 0.                                (D.18)

                                                   146
At g equal to the identity its solution is vc . The derivative in w is Id +Kg DNg (w), whose inverse is
a convergent Neumann series on a sufficiently small common ball by (D.15). The implicit function
theorem therefore gives a smooth solution of (D.18) in the group parameters.
     We specify the uniqueness argument because argument dilations move the support boundary.
First choose a fixed small ball on which the contraction estimates, and their nearby transformed
versions, hold. Taking the defect sufficiently small puts vc strictly inside a smaller ball. The uniform
boundedness of Ug then puts every Ug vc , for g near the identity, in the common contraction ball. It
satisfies (D.18) by conjugation. Uniqueness in that ball identifies it with the smooth implicit-function
branch. This uses the all-input definition of K, so no fixed support subspace has to be invariant
under dilation. Nor does it presuppose norm continuity of the group orbit of the unknown solution.
     Distributional differentiation of this smooth orbit shows that all iterated rotations Ωij =
yi ∂j − yj ∂i and dilations E = y · ∇, applied to vc , lie in X s . The rotation of tensor components
contributes only constant zeroth-order matrices, which may be subtracted in identifying these
derivatives. On every rescaled exterior annulus these fields span the scaled coordinate derivatives.
More explicitly, with r = |y|,                         ∑
                                        r2 ∂i = yi E +     yj Ωji .
                                                       j

Their iterates therefore control every scaled spatial derivative. The same identity is nondegenerate
on a neighborhood of |y| = 1. It follows that the correction is smooth across its support boundary
as well as on the exterior, and has symbol bounds of all orders with metric weight one and second
fundamental form weight two. These higher constants may depend on the higher symbol bounds of
dB and on the smooth source ϕ; their smallness is not required. The small base norm also keeps the
corrected metric positive definite.

Application to the beam seed. The rescaling, artificial interior extension, and verification of
the source hypotheses are carried out in Section 9.6, using (9.14)–(9.15). That application retains
the complete correction tail and proves approximation in any prescribed finite list of the original
data seminorms. Higher symbol bounds ensure smooth membership of each corrected datum; they
need not be uniformly small or give convergence at all orders for one fixed frequency exponent.
